BRIDAL MARCH

Chapter I
It was their hostess, Mrs. Braithwaite, who introduced
them. Rushing it as people do rush introductions in these
days and without any of the formality that attended such
rites and ceremonies in earlier, less hustling days. Tucking
a hand through Nicky's arm she led him, willy-nilly, across
the packed dance floor and up to a girl in black, who sat
alone in a high-backed chair.
"Darling, you don't know Nicholas Trent, do you? And
you must. He's one of my most particular specials. Nicky,
this is Celia Durham, and she's sweet. Now do be friends
and get on well together because I'm simply rushed to death
and I haven't a minute to spare for either of you. Be a lamb,
Nicky, and fetch the child an ice or something, will you?
And I'll see you both again before long. Do have a good
time, darlings."
"She's sweet," Celia said. "Only please don't bother about
the ice or something. I've eaten two already and I'm neither
hungry nor thirsty. If you want to sit down, there's room on
the arm of this chair. Unless you prefer to stand."
Nicky said:
"Personally, since I came for the purpose, I prefer to
dance. Especially as I went to considerable expense in order
to buy myself a pair of new pumps for the occasion." He
stood in front of her and held out a pair of inviting arms.
"Shall we? Or would you rather sit out?"
"I'd adore to dance," Celia said. She got up, shook out a
7
8
Bridal March
frilly black skirt and slid into his arms. "I don't get much
and I love it. And the band's marvelous."
"Lennie's parties are always a degree ahead of those given
by other people," Nicky said. He navigated a careful course
between a boy and a girl too engrossed in each other's eyes
to care where they went and a large man who pursued his
way with a dogged determination to get somewhere or
know the reason why, that probably accounted for the blue
ribbon decorating the white front of his shirt. "She does
things on a scale that's only equaled by her immoderate
language. I've never seen you before, have I? And why?"
"Poor relation," Celia Durham said concisely. "Too poor
to keep abreast of Lennie and not sufficiently poor to be
a hanger-on. It's the first time I've been to one of her parties.
The first time, as a matter of plain straightforward fact,
that I've possessed a frock sufficiently elegant to justify my
presence. I work in an office in the city. For forty shillings
a week. Interested?"
"Yes. Enough to want to know more anyway. We ought
to have a good deal in common for one thing. D'you live on
your forty bob a week?"
"Existing's nearer the mark, but have it your own way,"
Celia said. The music stopped on an interrogating minor
chord, and when the leader of the orchestra wouldn't give a
third encore, she turned and walked beside Nicky across the
emptying room and out into the garden beyond. "If you're
studying the conditions under which the poor working girl
lives, I'll give you all the details you want. I've done it
for three years. At thirty for six months."
Nicholas Trent said dryly:
"Thanks. You can keep the details. I know 'em. Every
littlest thing and then some. I work on a paper. For fifty
and what I can snaffle on the side. I was at Oxford with
Bridal March
9
Lennie's brother and she got me the job on one of her hus-
band's papers. For three hundred and sixty-four days I
stick an inquiring nose into other people's business, so that
still more other people shall have some nice spicy bits to
read with their eggs and bacon in the morning; and for the
same number of nights I waste a great deal of electric light I
can't afford, not to mention paper, ink and pens, endeavor-
ing to write a play that will prove to the world I'm a genius.
So far the effort has resulted in my complete failure even
to convince a skeptical editor that I am worth more to him
than the totally inadequate wage his minions fling at me
every Friday afternoon, but I live in hope. Occasionally, I
regret, on hope into the bargain, since money being made
to go round insists on carrying out its natural function in
life, and fifty bob doesn't go round far enough."
"And on the other day-the three hundred and sixty-
fifth," Celia said, "what happens then?"
"I take a day off, go shopping for pumps and meet
pretty ladies." He laughed down at her, and away in the
distance behind them the band sprang noisily to life.
"Thank God for the absence of programs. Let's go on,
shall we?"
They spent the greater part of the evening together, and
when the party was over he drove her back to Town in
a disreputably ancient two-seater that had obviously seen
better days. Bidding good-by to their hostess, she pulled
Celia into a corner and gave hasty advice.
"Nicky's fallen for you with a crash, so go steady. He's
a lamb, but he's no money, and what's more he'll never
have any. His people are the original church mice. Poor
and terribly proud. As a lover he'd be adorable, but as a
possible husband-well, I'd watch my step if I were you,
darling."
10
Bridal March
Going home in the soft warm darkness of a June night,
Celia remembered the advice and picked her way with cau-
tion. Which was a more difficult job than it might have
been, because obviously Nicky had no intention of watching
his own step. He said:
"Lennie's parties are always grand fun, but this has been
the best of the bunch. By streets. It wouldn't, I suppose,
be because you happened to be there, would it?"
"I should think it's doubtful," Celia said, remembering
Lennie's advice. "Although in any case it'll have a disastrous
ending unless you slow down a bit. Do you always drive in
this fashion?"
"Mostly," Nicky said, but he let up a fraction the pressure
of his foot on the accelerator. "And always when I'm ex-
cited. As I am now. Also you're shelving the issue. To-
night, for me, has been an occasion. I know it because
I suddenly feel I could write and I never feel I can write
anything but tripe unless something happens that tremen-
dously excites me. A really rough sea, for example. Or a vio-
lent thunderstorm. Or things like that. Two years ago our
house at home was struck by lightning, and I worked like
a maniac afterwards. For hours on end. When I get in to-
night I shall do it again. Because of you this time. D'you
usually have this disturbing influence on people? Or is it
a sign that I'm likely at any moment to fall very deeply
in love with you?"
Celia said, lightly, because her heart thumped:
"I think it's far more likely to be the result of that final
glass of champagne. If you remember I did warn you.
This is my road. The second house from the end. And
thanks a lot for bringing me home."
"You're being willfully misleading," Nicky said. He
brought the car, with a scream of protesting brakes, to a
Bridal March
II
standstill before the door of a silent darkened house.
"D'you mean-d'you honestly mean, Celia Durham-that
tonight hasn't meant anything in your life at all?"
"No," Celia said. She got out of the car and held out her
hand. "It's been a heavenly party, and I've loved it. Good-
night."
Next morning, at the office, one of the juniors came in to
tell her she was wanted on the telephone.
"Gentleman," Miss Collins said, briefly envious; and then,
her love of romance overriding even her envy, “Got a nice
voice he has and no mistake. Lucky to be some folks, I must
say."
Nicky at the other end of the 'phone spoke blandly, more
than a little pleased with himself and his ability to get out
of life that which he desired to get.
"If you're wondering how I found your number, I'll put
your mind at rest immediately by telling you that Lennie
gave it to me. Disapprovingly, I think, but that's beside the
mark. I just wanted you to know that I'll be waiting outside
your office at six tonight. Is that all right with you? I wanted
to make it lunch-time, but, unfortunately, my Chief seemed
to think it necessary to send me over to Shoreditch. A man
hanged himself by his braces there just before breakfast this
morning, and my paper likes titbits of that sort. However,
we can put in quite a bit of time from six onwards. I say,
you are there, aren't you?"
"Yes," Celia said. "I'm here only well, a little breath-
less. Is this sort of thing a sample of your usual method of
procedure?"
"Not altogether," Nicky said, unruffled. "And not at all
where girls are concerned. As a general rule girls don't
interest me. Whereas you do. Tremendously. There's some-
12
Bridal March
thing reliable about you and, of course, you're intelli-
gent-"
"Thanks," Celia said. "I'll tell my Chief your kind
opinion of me. Perhaps he'll give me a raise on the strength
of it." She was conscious of a queer throbbing excitement
that filled the air and made of a dull, rather drab city office a
place full of romance and thrills. "Is there anything else you
want to tell me? Because, even if you've nothing to do, I'm
busy."
"So'm I," Nicky said. "Isn't arranging one's future life
being busy? The details, however, can wait till I see you
at six."
"I can't get away at six," Celia said. It was untrue be-
cause the office staff left at five-thirty, and was prompted
only by a sudden desire to be contrary and difficult, and by
a still greater instinct for self-preservation. This thing that
had come so suddenly might hurt before one was through
with it. Hurt very badly into the bargain, and one didn't
run out to meet things that hurt. Life was difficult enough
at times anyway. One wanted so much, clothes and gayety
and the ability to live-well, as Lennie lived, for example-
and forty shilling a week only provided bare necessities. Left
no room at all for all the other lovely things that made up
Lennie Braithwaite's life. But at least one was heartwhole.
At least one could live for oneself, whereas if one let in
this other new thing- She said, playing what she already
knew to be a losing game, but playing it none the less: "I'm
sorry but I'm rushed tonight. We're busy. It'll probably be
at least half-past before I can get away."
"All right. I'll be there," Nicky said. "There's a place
I go to for dinner sometimes where I'd like to take you.
It's cheap, but the food's good, and afterwards we'll go
Bridal March
13
somewhere in the car. In any case I'll be waiting outside at
six-thirty. That be all right?"
"Yes-only-oh, make it a quarter past," Celia said, "and
I'll hurry." She told herself recklessly: "If I'm hurt-well, I
am, and that's all there is to it. In the meantime it's fun, so
why not make the best of it. And who cares about money
anyway?" She went back to the desk conscious of little Miss
Collins's hungrily romantic eyes and entirely indifferent to
the disapproval writ largely on the angular features of Miss
Archer, who was head of the "female" office staff, and who
had little use for men at any time, and none at all for those
who called up her assistants during office hours. Miss
Archer said acidly:
"Mr. Betteridge rang for you a few moments ago, Miss
Durham. Perhaps, if you've time to spare from your pri-
vate affairs, you'll take your book into his room now."
The sun was shining, Celia saw, when she went along
the corridor to Mr. Betteridge's room. It came through the
windows that overlooked the river and lay in little warm
yellow pools on the synthetic brown tiles that covered the
floor. Down below in the river barges and tugs fussed busily
to and fro. On one of the barges there was a woman wear-
ing round her head a brilliant scarlet scarf, and the patch
of color blazed in the sunshine, looking gay and reckless
and defiant amongst all the drab grays and browns and
blacks around it. The woman Celia could see was young,
tremendously alive and vivid, and suddenly a man came up
on to the untidy little deck, saw her standing there, pulled
her close to him and kissed her full on her red, reckless
mouth.
With her book in her hand, with Mr. Betteridge waiting
for her to take his dictation, Celia stood at the window
watching a little scene that, for no apparent reason, became
14
Bridal March
suddenly of tremendous importance. The man's action had
been rough, almost violent, but obviously the woman hadn't
minded. On the contrary, she'd even seemed to like it,
because for a quite perceptible moment she stood utterly
still in his arms, making no effort to get away from his
embrace, and in the end it was the man who let her go
and went on with the job he'd come on deck to do.
Watching them, Celia was aware of a sensation to which
until last night she'd been a complete stranger. Up till now
men hadn't troubled her. She'd met those she liked cer-
tainly, lots of them, but none capable of producing the
sensation that, ever since last night, had been steadily grow-
ing and deepening. None certainly from whom she would
have dreamt of tolerating-well, the sort of kiss the bargee
had given to the woman wearing the scarlet handkerchief.
She'd always been a little aloof where men were concerned,
priding herself on the fact that none of them found in her
the type of girl they could treat lightly, building up about
herself a little fence of reserve and dignity that said as
plainly as any words could have spoken: "Thus far, my
friends, but no further," and from the inside of the fence,
secure in her own little armored dwelling, she had watched,
amused sometimes, now and then not amused at all, the
ways of other girls. With no desire whatever to join their
ranks or to take part in the games they so recklessly played.
And now out of the blue had come Nicholas Trent and in
three short hours had destroyed all her carefully-built little
fences and left her standing in the open, so to speak, un-
protected and alone, and completely at his mercy. With,
moreover, no wish at all to rebuild the fence or to make
the slightest effort to evade his attentions. It was queer,
she told herself, suddenly remembering Mr. Betteridge wait-
ing in his room and wending her way through the in-
Bridal March
15
terminable corridors that led to him, the difference one man
could make to one's outlook on life. Disturbing too, so that
one felt a little light-headed and insecure, as one felt after
drinking too many cocktails on an empty tummy. But tre-
mendously exciting. So that even walking along the familiar
corridors became an adventure and the taking of Mr. Bet-
teridge's letters something that, quite mysteriously, became
astonishingly interesting and inspiring.
She was lucky, Celia told herself, to have Mr. Betteridge
to work for. All the other girls in the office, with the pos-
sible exception of Miss Jepson, who was the Chief's private
secretary and for that reason not of the common herd, en-
vied her the job of taking Mr. Betteridge's dictation because,
in addition to his being a real live wire and tremendously
keen and enthusiastic, his letters were always terribly thrill-
ing. He had charge of the "cruise" department and his
office was lined with pictures that showed people bathing on
white coral beaches, or sailing over dead-calm blue seas, or
marvelously clad in the last word in cruising clothes, drap-
ing themselves about steamer chairs on the decks of one of
the company's magnificent cruise ships. It was his job to
interview prospective passengers, to impress upon them the
magnitude of the pleasures offered them, to paint colorful
word-pictures of the countries they would see in their trav-
els, and, lest his word-pictures had not been sufficiently im-
pressive, his manner not altogether persuasive enough to
bring home to them the opportunities on which they most
surprisingly might consider turning their backs, to follow up
his interviews with carefully-dictated letters that said the
whole thing over again in an entirely different way. He was
clever at his job, too, and tremendously tactful, so that he
never stepped on people's over-sensitive toes. Never, for
example, drew the attention of a lady weighing sixteen stone
16
Bridal March
to the wonders of the swimming pools or the dance floors,
but, instead, laid particular stress on the excellence of the
bridge professional who accompanied every cruise, the com-
fort of the beds provided, and the perfection of the chef.
And arranging who should sit where, he was always very
careful to see that too-attractive young grass widows who
might be divorcées in private life should not share tables
with enterprising middle-aged gentlemen attached to unin-
teresting watchful wives. It always interested Celia pro-
foundly to see Mr. Betteridge at work. He was so keen to
succeed, not because success in any way increased his quar-
terly salary check, but just for the sake of success. He was
never really happy unless it were necessary to write to
prospective passengers regretful letters stating that every
cabin-more, every berth-was occupied; he reveled in
bookings, and took a personal interest in every passenger
with whom he had to deal. And in addition to his bookings
he had on his shoulders the responsibility of the ship's social
and pleasure life. It was Mr. Betteridge who saw to it that
the hostess accompanying every cruise had the gift of at-
tracting women as well as men, that the golf professional
knew how to be charming to ladies past their first youth,
that the swimming instructor could be trusted to discrimi-
nate between those who came to him for bona fide lessons
and those who merely saw in him a means to a pleasurable
end. It was he who arranged the social programs before
the sailing of every ship; who selected suitable prizes for
competitions; who whispered in the ear of the captain the
names of those passengers meriting special attention and
deference; who, carefully discriminating, caused to share
staterooms those he considered most likely also to share
similar tastes, so that gay young women preferring late
nights on deck and equally late mornings in bed might
Bridal March
17
not, on retiring, rouse from virtuous slumbers those who
sought pre-midnight beauty-sleep and elected to be little
rays of sunshine at the earliest of the ship's three breakfast
hours.
Celia, at the end of fifteen months' close association with
Mr. Betteridge, was getting wise, rapidly, to a great many
things about him. She knew, for example, that as an Ameri-
can thruster he had no use for the Englishman's more re-
served and possibly more dignified methods of attack, that to
him work and yet more work, spelled satisfaction, that com-
petition was to him the very breath of life, that he would
never, under any circumstances whatever, let anything in-
terfere with his job, and that he was probably the best cruise
passenger agent this side of of the Atlantic. He was short,
thin and wiry, with bright dark eyes and a trick of rolling
a cigar with his tongue even while he dictated his volumi-
nous correspondence; he did always just a little more than
was asked of him and he expected those working for him
to do the same; he regarded England as a "great little
country" but slow-"heck, how almighty slow"-and gave
what his work left of his heart and soul to his own native
land; he admired deeply and profoundly the majority of
English women, and, without having the slightest senti-
mental interest in her, he admired above all the rest, the
quiet, steady-going efficiency of his own particular typist.
When, still treading on the air provided for the purpose
by the telephone call of a tempestuous and impulsive young
man, she came into the office in which he awaited her, he
lost no time in unnecessary politnesses.
"There's this matter of the dance hostess for the Ameri-
cana, Miss Durham. And there's no time to lose either,
darn her. Now take this." He dictated swiftly, competently
for fifty minutes on end, rarely if ever contradicting him-
18
Bridal March
self, sliding from the subject of the dance hostess to that
of the pictures to be shown in a particular ship's cinema
theater, to the question of fancy-dress balls, to the change
of cabin allotted to the Duchess of Romsey, and to the
arrangements to be made for the accommodation of Lady
Malcolm Singleby's three prize pekes, punctuating the
whole with a series of running commentaries, the pun-
gency of which would have earned for him a fortune in
another walk of life. And at the end he sat back in his chair,
rolled his cigar from the right to the left side of his mouth,
and stared at her.
"How long've you been working for me, Miss Dur-
ham?"
"Fifteen months," Celia said, and added primly: "I hope
you've been satisfied, Mr. Betteridge?"
"You'd have been fired if I hadn't. I've no use for people
who don't satisfy me, and by this time you ought to know
that much."
"I do," Celia said. She shut her notebook and let a
flicker of a smile slide across the corners of her mouth.
"I said it because this morning I feel in the mood for
asking unnecessary questions. I saw a woman on a barge
just now. She wore a scarlet handkerchief round her head,
and a man came up from the bowels of the thing and kissed
her. I wanted to call out and ask her what it felt like, and
whether she liked it, because the sort of kiss they exchanged
wasn't what I'd have called-well-pleasant. I told you just
now I'm in an inquiring mood."
"Good," Charles Betteridge said complacently. "That's ex-
actly how I want you to feel, because there's a job ahead
for you and an inquiring mood's just, I guess, what's
needed. There's a ship on her way home. The Santa Rica.
Bridal March
19
She's been on a six weeks' cruise in the Mediterranean"
Celia said, interrupting:
"I know. She's got that new swimming instructor aboard.
O'Grady. She's calling at Marseilles tomorrow night. What's
wrong with her?"
"Nothing. And everything," Charles Betteridge said.
"Captain reports everything O. K. Five passengers, three
of them women, have rebooked for another cruise in her.
Twenty-seven have written four-page letters of gratitude
and appreciation from one port or another, and six have
left the ship at Alexandria. Three married couples. Two
middle-aged, the third youngish. They've sent rude letters
by air mail in which they don't say anything definite and
in which they hint the hell of a lot. Mainly that we pander
to immorality and that if we don't know the kind of pas-
sengers we carry it's time we did. Between Marseilles and
Southampton there's a matter of six days, and in six
days a woman with her head screwed on the right way
could find out a lot. Follow me?"
"The woman in question," Celia said with eyes that
shone, "being me? Is that what I'm supposed to follow?"
"Got it first time," Mr. Betteridge said, and leant across
his desk, facing her. "Now see here, Miss Durham. This
line can't afford to have folks saying about the conduct
of any of its ships the sort of things these folks hint at,
and the trouble is they only hint. If we could get 'em out
in the open and make 'em dot their 'i's' and cross their
't's,' we'd know where we stood, but folks that hint don't
come out in the open. And when they're questioned they
say we must've misunderstood what they meant. Which is
just plain baloney. See? So we've got to get things sorted
out the best way we can, and as I see it the best way is
to put you on board that ship when she gets to Mar-
20
Bridal March
seilles and leave the rest to you. You're smart and you're
reliable. I don't mind telling you the Chief and I don't ex-
actly see eye to eye on the matter, but that doesn't go for
much, and if he thinks I'm nuts sending a woman on the
job, he's at any rate willing to let me know how best
to run my own department and agree to your going. If
you pull it off and find out what's made these six respectable
folk leave the ship in a panic lest they get their reputations
tarnished, it'll be one big step up the ladder for you, and I'll
see you don't suffer by it. If you fail I'll be almighty dis-
appointed in you. What about it?"
"What time do I start tomorrow?" Celia said. "I suppose
I'll go by air"
"You suppose right. Up to a point," Mr. Betteridge said.
"Only you start tonight, and you go by air only as far
as Paris. There you pick up the train which'll get you in
an hour before the Santa Rica docks. The letters I've given
you will keep you occupied till lunch-time. When you've
done 'em you'll go to the cashier, and he'll give you your
ticker and fifty pounds in cash. You'll have a first-class
cabin on the Santa Rica and you'll do yourself well. Get in,
you understand, on everything that's going, make friends
with everybody who'll let you, gossip and be gossiped to, and
don't breathe a word to a soul as to who you are. Nobody
knows you and nobody need. Neither captain, nor officers,
nor passengers, see? Keep your mouth shut and your eyes
open and make the best of every second. What about
clothes? Got enough or d'you want 'em?"
"I want them," Celia said. "I get forty bob a week, Mr.
Betteridge," and the man at the table nodded.
"All right. I'm not arguing. Tell the cashier to give you
an order on the shops you want to trade with and rig your-
Bridal March
21
self up. And at six tonight you'll meet me and get your
passport-"
"Six," Celia said. She was on her way to the door and
suddenly she stood still. "I can't"
"I said six," Charles Betteridge said steadily, "because
that's the only hour I've got free the whole evening and six
it's got to be. If you've got a date that's just too bad, because
either you'll have to break it or I'll send the Wilkins girl to
Marseilles. You've only got to say the word, Miss Durham.
Just 'yes' or 'no.' Believe me, if you don't care to keep the
appointment, Miss Wilkins'll hop to it. Any minute. So it's
up to you."
"I'll meet you," Celia said. Her voice sounded flat and
dull, as though, suddenly, all the color had been washed
out of it, leaving it drably gray, and yet she knew she
couldn't possibly have refused to keep the appointment.
This was the chance of a lifetime. If she pulled it off-could
just get one finger on the trouble-it meant everything.
Charles Betteridge would see to that. And Nicky-he'd just
have to go, that's all. There was nothing else for it, anc. with
a chance such as this. She pushed out of her mind the pic-
ture of Nicky's face that had been occupying the forefront
of her brain ever since the night before, set her thoughts
sternly on the future. If she could make good there was no
knowing what might be the outcome of this job in the
Santa Rica. Mr. Betteridge hadn't the opinion of the aver-
age Englishman who considered a woman's place to be in
the home. In America women did anything, occupied really
big positions, even rose to be executives of monster or-
ganizations. So that if one could just prove to him one's
abilities, there was no knowing where the end would be. And
while Nicky was terribly attractive, while one wanted des-
perately to go downstairs at six-fifteen and see him waiting
22
Bridal March
for one at the door, he probably meant no more than the
average young man meant when he met a pretty girl at an
especially good party. And certainly he couldn't be allowed
-not, at all events, at the present stage of the game-
to take precedence of one's job when quite suddenly one
saw ahead not merely years spent in typing other people's
letters but the possibility of something big and great and
tremendously exciting.
And even if he were not to be just a passing ship, even if,
as he'd said last night and again over the telephone this
morning, their meeting was really to mean something to
both of them, he'd be sure to understand and to see her
point. Ambitious himself, terribly anxious to get on and
make good, he'd know in a second that nothing could be
allowed to stand in the way of a chance like this. And, of
course, she'd let him know. Telephone him at lunch-time
and explain. And perhaps, with luck, instead of meeting
her at the office he'd run the disreputable car out to Croy-
don to see her off. Having to give up the evening with him
wouldn't matter nearly so much if one could just see him
again and let him know that, despite all one's implied in-
difference, last night's party had really meant something
after all.
Back at her own typewriter there was such a rush to get
her letters through by lunch-time that she simply hadn't
time to spare for Nicky or for anything else, and it wasn't
until, choking down a cup of tea and a sandwich at the
nearest Lyons', with her ticket and fifty pounds of the
Company's money in her pocket, that she remembered she
didn't know the name of his paper. She called up Mrs.
Braithwaite because, of course, Lennie would know, and a
bland butler informed her regretfully that Mr. and Mrs.
Braithwaite had left home an hour earlier, and would be
Bridal March
23
away for a week; then spent a feverish ten minutes and all
her available coppers dialing the number of every news-
paper she could think of, only to draw a blank at the end.
One after another hurried operators told her to "hold the
line a minute, please," and when she'd held it interminably,
the ultimate information was always the same:
"Sorry, but we've no one of that name here."
And so in the end, since it was obviously impossible,
with so much to be done, to spend any more time dialing
unresponsive numbers, she had to give it up and turn her
attention to buying the necessary clothes, to rushing home
to fling things into suitcases and a dressing-bag, and to
getting out to Croydon in time to meet Mr. Betteridge for
dinner at six.
Mr. Betteridge stood her an excellent meal with a bottle
of really good champagne thrown in, but even then the
meal wasn't a success. The very best champagne, the most
delicious food had perforce to lose the perfection of its
savor when mixed up with it was the picture of Nicky
patiently waiting for hours perhaps outside the office door,
and the disquieting knowledge that for the first time in
one's life one was desperately in love with a man, who,
following that sort of a set-back, would probably take
excellent care never to meet one again.
Chapter II
LIFE on the Santa Rica was, of course, marvelous. There
was nothing that even the most fastidious passenger could
desire which wasn't provided, and the weather was perfect.
Even the Bay hadn't any terrors that cruise, and the only
trouble lay in the fact that, pry as she would, Celia could
24
Bridal March
find nothing on which she could put even a tentative finger
and say: "This, I think, is going to produce the informa-
tion I've come here to find." By the time the ship called
at Gibraltar she'd reached the border-line of a discreet
flirtation with the Captain, had established herself on terms
of friendly good-fellowship with the doctor, was in touch
with all the ship's gayeties, all her sports, all her more con-
spicuously-flagrant intrigues, had asked cautious questions
of all and sundry and, having found nothing, was contem-
plating with anything but pleasurable anticipation the
prospect of returning to the office minus the information
she had been especially sent to find. And then, when
she'd almost resigned herself to the disappointment of
returning empty-handed and a failure, luck played into
her hands. Leaving Corunna, it was hot and utterly still.
A heavy, moonless, starless night when it seemed as though
the ship moved alone through a world entirely her own,
and following the usual dance in the saloon Celia went
with half a dozen of the other passengers to the doctor's
cabin for a final drink. Later still, on the pretext of being
safely escorted to her cabin, she had spent a sentimental
twenty minutes staring over the ship's side into the im-
penetrable blackness in company with the Captain, then
at the entrance of her own particular alleyway had bidden
her escort good-night, and made her way alone along the
dimly-lighted corridor that led to her own stateroom.
There, almost opposite her doorway, she stopped short.
The handle of the cabin next to hers was being turned
very slowly and carefully, as though whoever turned it
wanted to make as little noise as possible, and-because on
board ship tact is a greater virtue by far than faith, hope
or charity-Celia slid into the open bathroom adjoining
so that she might not be seen by some one who, just then,
Bridal March
25
had probably no desire to see her or any one else-and,
waiting till the coast was clear, saw, without being seen,
the man who shut the stateroom door as silently as he had
opened it and, in the white and blue mess-dress that dis-
tinguished the ship's officers, made his way swiftly and
very quietly along the alleyway that led to the forward
deck and to the officers' quarters.
Back inside her own cabin, undressing for bed, Celia's
thoughts were busy. The stateroom next door was occupied
by a girl of perhaps her own age-a pretty, rather helpless
little thing with baby blue eyes and an appealing childish
mouth. The room she had herself had been the temporary
abiding-place of Mr. and Mrs. Averton, and the Avertons
had been two of the six who had left the ship at Alexandria.
Now, suppose Mrs. Averton or her husband, or both,
being ultra-respectable, God-fearing folk, had been sitting
up late and, coming back, had seen what she had seen,
and promptly drawn for themselves the conclusion she
had already reached? What then? Would they, filled with
righteous indignation, report to the Captain the thing they
had seen? Or would they discuss it with those of their
friends sharing their views on such matters, and in fear
of contamination seize the first available opportunity of
putting as much distance as possible between their own
unspotted reputations and the reprehensible character of
the pink-and-white baby-face who, as a veritable whited
sepulcher, resembled in no manner at all that which on
the surface she so obviously appeared to be? Because if
they had taken the latter course and had left the ship say-
ing nothing-as respectable and God-fearing people who
weren't either vindictive or spiteful might perhaps prefer
to do rather than get themselves involved in the possible
unpleasantness of a divorce case, since practically every

one on board knew that the first officer was a married man

Lying in bed Celia was conscious of a sudden irritation.
The first officer was nice-terribly nice. A big, quiet, ef-
ficient person, and not in the least the sort one could have
thought capable of that sort of behavior. And yet the man
in the alleyway had been most definitely the first officer.
His obvious admiration for the little fair girl, cruising in
the Mediterranean for reasons of health, was one of the
first bits of gossip Celia had been given after leaving Mar-

thing anyway. And it was terribly silly and risky because,
once it got out, there was an end of his job. The Company
was tremendously particular about the morals of its officers.
At all events in so far as their conduct on board ship was
concerned. It liked them to be good-looking, attractive and
popular, because women traveling alone liked nice-looking
men to dance attendance on them, but it wouldn't, for one
moment, stand any nonsense. Once it got out-as of course
it would have to do when she got home-he was done
for as far as the Santa Rica went, or, for that matter, as far
as any of the Company's other ships were concerned, so
that it was silly to do things of that sort, and Celia had no
patience with men who jeopardized their chances. She'd
had time, since she came on board, to get a line-up on her
own feelings and to be glad she hadn't let sentiment inter-
fere with her own chances. Sentiment was all very well in
its proper place, but it had to be kept there, and even love
couldn't be allowed to racket all over the place and to
play havoc with one's future. Nicholas Trent was sweet,
Bridal March
27
of course. Equally, of course, one had fallen for him with

Chapter III
BACK in the office Mr. Betteridge said complacently:
"Well-if that's all I guess there's no real harm done.
Thanks, Miss Durham. I'll keep this little job in mind for
future reference and in the meantime I've made arrange-
ments for you to work exclusively for me in future. There's
a busy season ahead and it seems to me you might as well
learn to shoulder a bit of extra responsibility. That O.K.
with you?"
"It's grand," Celia said, and added, her thoughts clearing
because the last three days had been worrying her badly:
"I needn't say I'm glad you're satisfied."
Playing the game from both sides of the net hadn't been
easy. For hours she'd argued the whole thing out from
every visible angle, and never once had she seen a way
by which she could be loyal to the Company and to Mr.
Betteridge's trust in her, and at the same time safeguard
the job of a man guilty of nothing worse than a perfectly
legitimate desire to comfort his own wife. Obviously her
path was clear. She could tell Mr. Betteridge exactly what
she had seen and there the matter passed out of her hands.
Jim Archways would lose his job, but that wasn't her fault,
and in any case, she told herself resentfully, love shouldn't
be allowed to butt in on duty. It all came back exactly to
the same starting-point. Love was all very well in its proper
place, but love and duty wouldn't mix and there was no
excuse for even trying to make them mix. If she'd let
Nicholas Trent's charm butt in across her job-well, she
certainly wouldn't be enjoying Mr. Betteridge's trust, and
her chances of really making good in the office would be
as vague and problematic as Nicky's intentions, so what
Bridal March
33
was the use of taking love into consideration? On the other
hand, however, it was hard luck on a perfectly nice couple
if Jim Archways should lose his job merely because she told
the whole truth and nothing but the truth, when it would
do her no harm and would certainly do none to the Com-
pany either, if she tempered truth with tact and a modicum
of generosity and kept his name out of it altogether.
And in the end sentiment won. She told herself irritably
because she knew she shouldn't even listen to sentiment
at all:
"I've got to tell what I know. I've got to, however much
I may hate it, and if he loses his job, as of course he will,
well, no one can blame me. He oughtn't to take chances
with it any more than I did. People who take chances
with their job ought to lose them." And then an hour be-
fore the ship docked, five hours before she would find her-
self sitting in Charles Betteridge's office giving a report of
her stewardship, a deck-steward brought her a blue en-
velope on a tray.
"Message for you, Miss," he said cheerfully, and for a
minute she didn't even bother to open it. It was from the
office, of course. A message from Mr. Betteridge probably
making things infinitely worse than they already were;
then at last she slit open the envelope and everything in
the world changed as though a master-hand painting a
picture had suddenly decided to splash a glowing yellow
sun into a background of gloomy grays and blacks. Because
the message was from Nicholas Trent, and to send it must
have cost a fortune. It said:
"Have discovered you at last stop shall meet boat train
tomorrow and expect detailed explanation whys and where-
fores stop am worn to a shadow with anxiety stop make
no arrangements for evening without consulting me Nicky"

Bridal March
And when she had read it once she went back to the
beginning, read it all through again very carefully, and
then folded it up and tucked it away in her bag. What a
fool he was. What a fool and how recklessly extravagant.
But what a dear fool and how glorious to be extravagant
like that just once in a way. She would speak to him about

of fifty shillings weekly-of spending almost the entire

how much simpler and easier and more straightforward

sky had come the blazing ray of brilliant sunshine that was
Nicky's message.
And quite suddenly, too, it flung its penetrating light
over the difficulties surrounding the matter of the first
officer and his misdemeanors. Because while, of course, one
must do one's job one couldn't let zeal for self-advancement
utterly spoil the lives of two people whose only sin lay in
loving each other, and so, after a very brief plan of action,
she had hit on a story for Mr. Betteridge that told the
truth in so far as it was necessary for him to know it and
left the first officer out of it altogether. It was in the end
quite a simple matter to tell the story convincingly and
even to make of it something not really worth the trouble
of telling at all.
"I gather," Celia told Mr. Betteridge, facing over the

Avertons and their friends were quite nice people, they

the facts of the case." She warmed up to the story as she

It didn't, but since there was nothing else to do about it,
it had to suffice, and the week-ends went on. Through the
heat of stuffy breathless August, through September's golden

worked at his job in Fleet Street, interviewed would-be
suicides or the successful suicide's sorrowing relatives, wrote
up vivid accounts of fires, of explosions, of accidents, trailed
from one end of London to the other and back in search of
"copy," and drew in return his fifty shillings weekly. And
on Monday, Wednesday and Friday evenings he sat in the
Chelsea bed-sitting-room that comprised his London home,
and wrote snappy little articles for the production of those
"extra fivers" the need for which seemed to grow with each
passing day, and in his spare time worked on the play that
was, he glibly assured himself, one day to make his name
famous throughout the English-speaking world. Celia,

merits of Luxury Cruisers Ltd.; twice she had, alone and

relying far too heavily on their femininity to tide them
over errors, too secure in the knowledge of their appeal,
and with almost invariably the hope that the job might
be only a temporary affair-hedging, so to speak, the gulf
between the end of schooldays and the beginning of mar-

more of its intricacies than was absolutely incumbent upon
them in order to make sure of their weekly pay-checks.
They were temperamental creatures, too, on the whole, ad-

were "affairs" either in or out of the office that inter-

Chapter V
THEY saw it almost simultaneously-a little low house

55

56
Bridal March
quite obviously, been a formal garden, and in the other
corner there was a second door that led through to a little
square hall with a dining-room on one side, and at the end

Upstairs the rooms were much the same as those below,

Bridal March
57
and butter and found the cake. And then, sitting beside each

It must have been nearly three o'clock when, back in

Bridal March
it I'm dead sober. The last drink I had was the glass of

59
moment I saw it. Definitely and absolutely. I was so certain

ours, and we've got to have it.' She pulled her hands out of

anything as wonderful. In the world. Let's get married and
take it, Nicky."
Chapter VI
THE taking of the house proved a far simpler matter, curi-
ously enough, than the actual getting married, because the
owner who had had the place on his hands for nearly ten
years, was only too thankful to find tenants asking little
more than the privilege of living in it and paying him rent.
Hitherto prospective tenants had asked inconvenient ques-
tions as to water, sanitation and other domestic details,
and receiving his guarded replies, had wandered in search
of other houses, less picturesque perhaps but equally less
60
Bridal March
difficult to manage. This young couple, on the other hand,
asked none of the usual awkward questions and were so
obviously delighted with their find that he bitterly regretted
the bribe to which he'd descended in order to get a tenant
at any price before the place fell to bits before his eyes,
and cursed himself for a fool in that he'd dropped the rent
from its original price of fifty pounds per annum "exclu-
sive" to that of forty, the landlord paying all rates and
taxes.
"Forty pounds a year," Nicky said, producing pencil and
paper, "is, roughly, three pounds, six shillings and some-
thing a month, or equally roughly sixteen and sixpence a
week. At the present moment I am paying eighteen and six-
pence a week for the foul dump called by courtesy a flat,
and you, I should imagine, must be paying at least double
that sum, so that at the very outset, marriage must be an
economy."
"Both yours and mine are furnished," Celia reminded

"'Furnished' may be the landlord's description but
scarcely mine. The dead remains of a bankrupt's stock sup-
plied what constitutes the furniture in my room I imagine,

about all day, and from what I can gather there are firms

down-"
"And ten shillings a week till you die!" Celia said.

Bridal March
61
given belfry, but I wonder if any two people have ever

But if the acquisition of the house presented no difficulty,

Nicky, apprised of the proposal, said:

Bridal March
I'll be thirty pounds to the good when I come back, and

And so, to Lennie Braithwaite's furious indignation and

side hotel. At the end of which period Nicky returned to

Chapter VII
THE original six weeks of the trip stretched itself into nine
before it ended, and Celia had a marvelous time. She had
to work, and work hard, because the office in general and
Mr. Betteridge in particular had no intention of paying
any of its employees money for nothing, but her evenings
for the most part were free and she made the most of
them. She made friends easily too, and in the office, while
they laughed at her "English accent" and chaffed her about
her English habits, the men and women who made up the
staff of the New York and Boston branches went out of
their way to show her the ropes of their respective "home
towns" and to send her back to England with pleasant
memories of her American trip.
It shocked her a little sometimes when, in the middle of
a party, her fingers came in touch with her wedding ring,
and she remembered that she was a married woman. It
wasn't, of course, that she ever forgot Nicky. Somehow,
once having known him it wasn't possible to forget him
even for a moment, but it was queer how misty, how dream-
like was the memory now, of those forty hectic hours of
her honeymoon. For the first two days on board ship she'd
Bridal March
67
thought, wretchedly, that life could never again be what
it had been before the night she'd spent in Nicky's pas-
sionate arms. Nicky had been so wonderful. So strong and
yet so tender. Taking all she had to give as his right and
yet making of the gift something so great, so transcendent,
that, at even the memory of it long, long afterwards, a little
quiver of sheer ecstasy would quicken the blood in her
veins and send her almost dizzy with the happiness of
remembrance. If the Aquitania had called at a single port
after leaving Cherbourg when she'd known what it was to
lie awake alone thinking of Nicky, she would have flung
Common-sense to the four winds and have left the ship
then and there. But at twenty-four, with the Atlantic under
one's feet, with the glow of Adventure lending wings to
one's imagination, and knowledge that, after all, six weeks
is only six weeks and that anticipation multiplies a thou-
sandfold the joys of the present, it was only natural and
reasonable that in the thrill of today memory of yesterday
should lose some of its tragedy and take instead merely
the misty beauty of a lovely thing to which sooner or later
one will most assuredly return.
And America, certainly, did her best to help her forget
or at least to help the time that separated her from Nicky
to pass more quickly. It was, Celia told herself again and
again, a marvelous country. So cocksure of itself, such a
supreme egoist and yet withal so childish, so pleased with
itself and its progress. And so hospitable. So ready and
anxious to welcome this visitor from overseas, and to give
her of its best.
Its egotism amused even while it irritated her. These
people of the new world, of this infant continent, that had
nothing to use as a bolster for itself save its own modern
progress, that had behind it neither ideals nor tradition,
68
Bridal March
that could look back on the history of merely a couple of
hundred years, were so certain of themselves. So convinced
of their own place in the sun, a little scornful and pitying
of others not fortunate as they themselves were fortunate
in being permitted by Fate to be born Americans. They
spoke of England as a "great little country," tolerantly,
for the most part, as one speaks of a nice child deprived
by circumstances over which it has no control of the
advantages granted to others, but had no patience with
its ideals and its traditions. One did things because it was
advantageous to oneself, not because it was either "good
form" or the "right thing." One looked primarily, after
number one, knowing full well that number two would
be equally engaged on a similar tack, and always one knew
that, when it came to a show-down, there was no product,
no efficiency, no brains, no modern gadget that could
touch the products and efficiency, the brains and modern
gadgets of God's own country.
Celia learnt, too, that the phrase, "God's own country"
wasn't merely a phrase. They believed it, these citizens of
this great new cocksure world. Were utterly sure of the
fact that they had been chosen by the Almighty to repre-
sent His brains, His forethought, His initiative, and that He
had Himself designed for them and for theirs the country
in which it had pleased Him to set them. They loved to
show it off, to point to its buildings, its towering skyscrapers,
its modern apartment houses, its air-conditioned theaters
and flats. When Celia told them of her own cottage-the
Little House that had neither water, nor modern drainage,
nor electric light-they said: "Why, it must be just too
cute," but one knew that not one of them would ever
have lived in it, as it was, for more than forty-eight hours,
and then only because there was no other roof available
Bridal March
69
by way of shelter. They would have taken the house cer-
tainly, reveling in it, in its antiquity and its old-world
charm, and would instantly have set to work to modernize
it, not content to rest for one moment until its walls held
invisible hot-water pipes, until its skirting boards bristled
with electric light and power plugs, until, following the
example set by the majority of America's older generation,
its age would be so marvelously camouflaged as to be
practically indiscoverable at all.
But if its cocksureness irritated her now and then, if,
over and over again, she longed to break into their egotism
with the reminder that England had been a great Empire
for centuries before Columbus had inadvertently stumbled
on America, its efficiency was a never-ceasing source of
wonder, and the part its women played in the national and
economic scheme an eye-opener. Accustomed to the tolera-
tion with which, in nine cases out of ten, women in business
are still regarded in any English office, the knowledge that
women in America ranked on an equality with men gave
her a definite jolt, and, too, an added interest to a job
in which, every day, the interest was already accumulating
by leaps and bounds. Sent by Mr. Betteridge one morning
to interview an official at one of the principal banks, a
woman, gracious, dignified, tremendously efficient, had re-
ceived her, talked with her, mystified her a little, since even
for a New York receptionist she seemed more than usually
poised and knowledgeable, until a chance word had proved
her the official in question. And they were all so smart, so
well-dressed. She thought of a word far more prevalent in
the States than in England and knew it fitted these graceful,
capable American women. They were elegant, carrying
themselves well, wearing good corsets, having their hair
carefully dressed and tended, their nails manicured and pol-
70
Bridal March
ished, their faces bearing obvious traces of regular massage,
and she compared them with, for instance, Miss Jepson and
Miss Archer in the office at home. Who were doubtless
equally competent and efficient, but who indubitably looked
it. Who lost, in their efficiency and competence, all the
charm of their womanhood and the attractions of their sex.
To whom Mr. Betteridge, for example, could quite com-
fortably and with perfect reliance have entrusted the conduct
of their respective departments but in neither of whom he
would have caught even a passing glimpse of a woman
capable of inspiring physical desire.
She noticed, too, with a little shock of discovery, that
of the women who worked the majority, young or middle-
aged, were married, wearing their wedding-rings or not as
they chose, working for the most part under their maiden
names, but making no secret of their married life. At the
lift gates at night Celia would see husbands waiting. Some-
times, arriving early, they would come up actually into the
office and claim their respective wives and no one minded.
Heads of departments, nodding "good-night" to "Miss
Jones" or "Miss Brown" or "Miss White," nodded equally
affably to the husband of "Miss Jones" or "Miss Brown"
or "Miss White," should he chance to be waiting, and
there was no thought of questions being asked. Celia re-
membered the case of Miss Jepson's predecessor in the
office at home, and the fuss there'd been when she'd stated
quite frankly that she meant to work after her marriage,
and that she hoped the office would keep her on. The
office hadn't kept her on, of course, and Miss Archer had
made no secret of her ideas on the subject. They followed
very much the lines of Mrs. Durham's ideas, and even the
Chief, who belonged to the old school, had laid down the
law very firmly on the subject of married women working.
Bridal March
71
Mr. Betteridge, being an American, didn't share his ideas
at all. Marriage meant nothing to him provided it didn't
interfere with efficiency, but even so, Celia hadn't mentioned
the fact of her own marriage. Some day, perhaps, she'd tell
him, but not yet. Not now at all events, when, clearer
than ever before, she had caught a glimpse of a future that
with luck might perhaps be hers, because if these American
women could do it-why not she herself? If, despite the
handicap of their sex and there was no getting away from
the fact that in England sex in business was a handicap-
they could climb to the top of the business, then why
couldn't she climb there too? Mr. Betteridge, being an
American, would ignore her sex because it suited him to
ignore it and would, acting as a ladder, certainly do every-
thing in his power to help her; and if American women
could combine marriage and business and yet, as many of
them could and did, make an obvious success of both, then
there was no logical reason why she, with her head screwed
on tightly, walking warily and watching her step, shouldn't
do the same. And, with Nicky to live with in off-duty
hours, to play with, to love and be loved by, with success
only one short step ahead of one, and always beckoning one
on, how marvelous-how perfectly wonderful life could be!
It was with all her ideals beginning with an illustrated
initial letter, with all the pointers America had given her
carefully stored in the pockets of her brain and in the pages
of her rapidly-filling notebook, and with Success barely out
of her reach and every day coming a little closer, that at
the end of her nine weeks she went back to England. To
leave behind the hectic life of bustling New York City,
and to take up, with all its puzzles and intricacies, its
stumbling-blocks, its joys and its sorrows, its difficulties and
its ecstasy, life with Nicky in the Little House.

Bridal March
Chapter VIII
THERE were, as Celia discovered before she'd been Mrs.
Nicholas Trent for more than six months, a great many
things to learn of which, as Miss Celia Durham, she'd had
no knowledge whatever, and yet which were incumbent
upon every phase of her married life.
It was, for example, one thing to be engaged to a man
and quite another to be married to him, especially when,
rushed to death at the office, one came home tired out and
edgy, and found a husband tired and edgy too. Or, which
was equally difficult, leaving the office with one thought
uppermost in one's mind which was to make for oneself
a cup of very hot tea, have a boiling hot bath and go to
bed with a book, to find a husband home from his job early,
freshly bathed and clad in glad rags, waiting with theater
tickets to greet one and ready to drive back to town for a
gay evening. It was difficult, too, with one's thoughts
snowed under by cruise data, with one's entire memory given
up to office details, to remember meals to be ordered and the
thousand and one domestic details incumbent upon the
successful running of a house. Janet, maid-of-all-work,
was cheap and therefore desirable up to a point, inasmuch
as at least she swept and dusted after a fashion, made beds
and washed up, so that one was not greeted, on one's home-
coming, by the depressing sight of dirty dishes and unmade
beds. But she had no brains at all, and what Celia herself
forgot to order remained unordered, with the result that
evening meals always retained an element of uncertainty.
Which was amusing once or twice, but lost its ability to
amuse after a while.
And there were days, wearying, exasperating days, when
Bridal March
73
the daily journey to and from town became a nightmare.
When the car went wrong, when tires having been as
hard as nails at seven-thirty the previous night and un-
touched in the shed ever since, went mysteriously flat
overnight, when fog or ice or snow made even the drive
to the station a twice-daily horror, when water froze in
the well and the oil-man forgot to call, when the fire in the
beautiful ingle-nooks smoked in the wind or sizzled dis-
mally in the teeming rain, when one room at an exorbitant
rent in Chelsea or Hampstead resembled a distant Paradise,
and when nothing in the world went right or showed the
slightest prospect of ever going right again. When separa-
tion, or even divorce, seemed the only possible way out
of the tangle into which life had inexplicably tied itself
and when, if the sun did break through the clouds for
a moment, it was only to show the Jacob's ladders which
are, as every one knows, the forerunners of further bad
weather to come.
But if the black days were conspicuous by their presence,
there were gay days too. When one remembered to give
the order to the butcher, the baker and the candlestick-
maker, when lamps gleamed through the darkness, when
the big low sitting-room was exactly the room one had
pictured in one's dreams, when the car, the office and the
day generally ran on greased wheels, when one knew that
all the horrors of days that didn't run on greased wheels
were worth while and that anything, anything and every-
thing was worth while, and much, much more than worth
while, provided one could be married to Nicky and live in
the Little House. Because the Little House was marvelous.
It had its drawbacks, of course, but then every house had
at some time or other, and if the distance from the office
became, on occasions, an interminable pilgrimage only to
74
Bridal March
be undertaken as an act of profound penitence for sin, it
also meant that once inside the house one was free of inter-
ruption and certain of privacy.
On gay days-and one had to admit, honestly, that the
gay days did predominate-it was wonderful to wake up
in the morning in the big, old-fashioned, four-poster bed
with the hangings of blurred cretonne that really looked
almost like old-fashioned dull chintz, and lie for a stolen
ten minutes in Nicky's arms, counting one's blessings. Won-
derful to have Nicky to shout to across the landing while
one dressed, to be able to set off together in the rackety
old car, to meet again at the station at night, to travel back
together, and hand-in-hand to come through the little gate
that persisted in squeaking despite repeated applications
of the oil-can, into the friendliness and warmth of the
Little House. Wonderful to slip out of an office frock into
something pretty and fluffy and feminine, to sit opposite
Nicky at the candle-lit "reproduction" refectory table, to
talk over the day's work and its trials and difficulties, and
to know that, when it was time to go to bed, one would
realize the supreme ecstasy of lying in Nicky's arms, of
hearing him say for the hundredth thousandth time all
the things one had ever wanted to hear, of knowing that,
for time and eternity, nothing, nothing in the world could
ever come between them and the love they had found
together.
And in the office things were getting definitely more
interesting. Celia had her own office now instead of a desk
in the corner of Mr. Betteridge's room. It was small, cer-
tainly, so that with its original occupant and a couple of
clients it became positively crowded, but it was her own,
and when there was room, the Chief had promised her a
Bridal March
75
larger one. He had spoken to her really nicely following
her return from the States.
"Mr. Betteridge tells me you have been of extraordinary
assistance to him, Miss Durham, and that he finds you
most able and competent. I need not tell you that his
report of approval and appreciation has made a very defi-
nite impression on my mind and that when there is a
chance of promotion your name will not be overlooked."
"I like working for Mr. Betteridge," Celia had said
simply, and then: "Thank you for telling me what he
thought of me, Sir Henry. I loved the job."
So that, taking things all in all, life was both pleasant
and enjoyable. It had its bad days, but then every one
had those, and the good ones amply made up for them.
Nicky was making money too now. Not a lot, certainly,
but the occasional "fivers" were a great deal more plentiful
than they had been in the past, and he had received the
munificent rise of ten shillings weekly, so that with his
regular salary of £150 a year, plus Celia's weekly four
pounds and the occasional "fiver," they had at least an
income sufficient for their immediate needs, with a little
left over for the lighter side of life. And even Lennie,
making a belated wedding-call on the tenants of the Little
House one sunny spring Sunday, could find no real cause
for complaint or commiseration. She said, grumbling:
"When I think what you could have made of your life
if you'd a grain of sense I feel sick, but I suppose you're
happy. You've no right to be, but you look it."
"We are," Celia said. It was late April and her eyes
rested contentedly on the pretty room with its bowls of
primroses and daffodils and hyacinths, with its log fire
burning in the huge open hearth, with the silver that had
been Lennie's own wedding present gleaming in the late
76
Bridal March
afternoon sunshine. Outside Nicky was exhibiting proudly

a thing in the world I really want that I haven't got. If
only life can go on for a bit as it's started I shan't have a
solitary fault to find with it anywhere."
But as it happened, life didn't go on as it started. Celia
had been Nicky Trent's wife for a little more than two
years when, ten minutes after her return from lunch one
day, Nicky called her on the 'phone. He was so excited
that his words tumbled over each other and were almost
indistinguishable, but in the end she got the facts of the
case. Shorn of superlatives and the unnecessary additions
prompted by breathless astonishment they mounted to this.
A godfather in far-away Australia of whom Nicky had
heard nothing for interminable years, whose bare existence,
in fact, he had completely forgotten, had died. And on
his death-bed had remembered his godson to the comfort-
able tune of one hundred and sixty-three thousand pounds.

And so, ultimately, a plan of campaign was reached that,
if it wasn't by any means the arrangement Nicky had
hoped it might be, was at least workable. The Little House
was to be kept on, modernized up to a point, so that for
the future there would be no necessity for Nicky to pump
up the water from the well outside the kitchen door, and
there would be electric light. Nicky had made rather an
issue of electricity, and because she had gained her own
point, Celia had given way at last, not very happily, but
at least gracefully. There would be two good maids instead
of Janet, and a car each in place of that which for years
past Nicky had affectionately termed "Old Faithful." There
would be a tiny service flat in town for use after theaters
and late nights, and while Nicky gave up his days to the
play, which, now that the paper was a thing of the past,
would certainly make him famous throughout the literary
world, Celia would stay on at the office to occupy the new
room; on the glass door of which discreet black lettering
proclaimed to those sufficiently interested to take notice
that the room was occupied by "Miss Celia Durham. West
Indian Section."
Chapter X
Ar the end of a year even Nicky had to admit that the
plan worked. Admittedly the play wasn't finished but, as
he explained to himself as well as to Celia, "plays weren't
86
Bridal March
written overnight," and one simply couldn't hurry things.
Working against time or even working at set hours killed
creative work, was responsible for half the hopelessly-
impossible plays and books palmed off on a non-critical,
far-too-easily-satisfied public. One just had to work when
one felt like it, and in the meantime life was lots of fun.
It was nice to have money, to be free, and young and popu-
lar, to find invitations to some party or other in every
morning mail, and if letting Celia go off to work seemed
all wrong, she at least liked it and was happy.
They had made friends now in the village. The news
that "those young Trents" had bought the Little House
and were, moreover, spending a great deal of money on
improvements, soon leaked out, and people began to sit
up and take notice of them. Lady Emily Torridge, who
"ran" the village, spoke of them almost enthusiastically.
She had called, she told Miss Susan Copley, because "one
really must be reasonably agreeable in a little village of
this size," and though she had found Mrs. Trent out, Mr.
Trent was at home. He had been in the garden when she
arrived and had proved so charming, "a really delightful
young man, my dear Susan," that ultimately she had yielded
to his persuasions and stayed to tea.
"These modern young people are so amazing," Lady
Emily said. "Actually, he told me, his wife works in the
City. Is a junior partner, I think, or something like that in
a large shipping firm, and tremendously keen; while he
writes. And they must have plenty of money because they

to be charming, and I, personally, am very glad that I turned
my back on old-fashioned prejudice in connection with
strangers and called there. I am sure we shall find them a
great acquisition to the social life of the village."

87

Chapter XI
As it happened, virtue had what Mr. Betteridge at all

He said, outlining his scheme:

Chapter XIV
CAROLYN ABвот called herself an artist. Less for the reason
that she painted pictures than for the excuse it gave her
to live the kind of life she preferred to live amongst the
people with whom she preferred to associate. It is true that
while she made her home in a Greenwich Village studio
rather than in the uptown Park Avenue apartment to which
the accident of birth and her father's money entitled her,
the method of her living bore little or no resemblance to
that of the majority of the men and women who made of
her house Liberty Hall in every sense of the word. She
Bridal March
117
had, in the first place, three excellent French maids, who
ministered competently to her wants, who served perfect

liked even more the creature comforts to which from her
childhood she had been accustomed, and she employed a
French cook and parlor-maid only because a French cook
is one of the few human beings in existence capable of
creating the Phenix of a satisfying and tastefully-prepared
meal for six from the ashes of that originally ordered for
one, and because a French parlour-maid, instructed at the
eleventh hour to reorganize her entire table in order to take
care of people invited to partake of a meal only because,
despite the announcement that luncheon or dinner is served,
they make no attempt to leave, will grumble to her heart's
content for five minutes, and afterwards forget all about it.
She drove a large, opulently-upholstered car, or had it
driven for her by a uniformed chauffeur, and her clothes
were so simple and unostentatious that every one knew
without being told the enormous sums she paid for them.
The studio itself was a joke even on rare occasions to
Carolyn herself because apparently the only thing it
lacked was the remotest semblance of her profession, and
from morning till night it was full of men and women, the
majority of whom she only knew very slightly or didn't
know at all, but who came because, being parasites and
sponges by nature, they liked the comfort it provided, and
the free and ungrudged meals that were invariably forth-
coming at all hours of the day or night.

wine, eating her food, one or two genuinely liked her even

Island and the New Jersey side got under one's skin and

Nicky, of course, loved it. But then he was never tired.
He'd nothing to do save join in with the crowd and enjoy

He'd taken to writing again, had turned out a couple of

Over and over again, Celia caught herself envying him

"I'm just dead," she said. "Physically and mentally. And

Bridal March
Why am I deadly flat and stale and tired like this? I get up

Chapter XV
THEY sailed for Cuba at the end of October, just when
all New Yorkists with money enough to pay fares are
planning to get away out of town for the winter in search
of the sunshine their own city lacks, and when Havana
is decking herself out in her best for the harvest to be
reaped from the winter tourists who swarm off the big
cruise-ships, throng her streets and crowd her hotels.
Celia herself was glad to go, acutely conscious of the
fact that she was rapidly reaching the end of her physical
tether. It wasn't reasonable, she told herself, hunting des-
perately for any excuse to account for spiritlessness and
lassitude, to expect people to live the sort of life New York
lived and stay sane. Even Nicky had flagged a bit towards
the end, was feeling more than a little the strain of con-
tinuous late nights, of the ceaseless frenzied rush to crowd
into each hour the last and final minute's worth of pleas-
ure. Or what, in this hectic new world, passed, it seemed,
for pleasure. When Celia gave him the date for their
leaving, so that he could go ahead with arrangements for
giving up the flat, he'd even sighed a little with relief.
"Glad to go?" Celia said, and he nodded.
"Just a bit. This place it's terrific at first. Sweeps one
off one's feet, so that one feels like a god. Capable of any-
thing. So saturated in the vital energy oozing out of it
everywhere that nothing seems beyond one's reach. I saw
visions of doing marvelous things. Almost I think that
when I first came here I knew the meaning of what tem-
peramental novelists call 'inspiration.' Every one was doing
so much-getting on with things. Satisfied with nothing
but a place actually in the sun, and refusing to tolerate the
132
Bridal March
mediocre in anything. The fact that I don't agree with
half the things they think here represent the top of the
tree, is neither here nor there. The thing that really matters
is that in this country people don't stagnate as they do
at home. They're forever heading all out for something, and
if they fail, well, at least they've tried to make a place for
themselves. The trouble is one gets tired so soon. Spiritu-
ally tired, if you know what I mean. I suppose it's because
by birth we belong to something so different, and once the
gilt is off the gingerbread it loses its appeal for us. When
I first came I thought I'd do wonders. Re-write the play-
get off a dozen good articles; and after the first flash-in-
the-pan I've done nothing. Physically, I'm as hard as nails
and as fit as a fiddle, but spiritually I'm done. My moral
resistance is exhausted for one thing. Over here values go
differently. On everything. I never thought myself an old-
fashioned, out-of-date moralist till I came over here and got
mixed up with Clifton's crowd."
"Clifton's crowd," Celia said, "aren't typical of America,
Nicky. Or of New York. I'm not holding any brief for
them, but that's only fair. You'll find just the same atti-
tude anywhere if you happen to get into that particular

ing. Or perhaps exciting's a better word. One gets a thrill

smart to slap convention in the face, and prefer to ignore
Bridal March
133
the fact that when a man likes to look at a woman without

In the bathroom that in the absence of another available
room Nicky used for a dressing-room, Kim had laid out
immaculately his dress clothes: his dinner-jacket, soft
pleated shirt, and the stiff-winged collar Nicky had refused
to change for the prevailing soft silk affair Rodney Clifton
and his crowd wore, trousers hanging from a clip, his hand-

did duty for a dressing-table. Kim was a good servant, and
they'd miss him when they had to do without him. Nicky,
changing tweeds for dress clothes, wondered if they could
persuade him to come with them. Servants, he'd been told,
were the devil to get in Cuba. Education and American
tourists had ruined them, giving them an inflated sense of
their own value, so that neither the Jamaican negro nor the
Cuban were any good any longer. And Kim was excellent.

137
He ran the house so comfortably, so quickly and efficiently

Carolyn's French servants ran hers.
He wished, without really worrying very much about the
matter, but conscious of the wish's existence, that he hadn't
dropped into the habit of going to see Carolyn quite so
often. At first he'd gone, because he was sorry for her,
which was really the reason he continued to go, but he
had a vague impression that Carolyn wasn't attributing his
visits to pity, that she was giving to them a personal value
he'd never meant them to have. She'd taken to being alone
when he came now, to giving orders to the maid that she
was to be left alone.
"If any one comes, say that I'm engaged, Francette. Until
Mr. Trent leaves I don't want on any account to be dis-
turbed." And Francette would smile. Discreetly, but very
understandingly, scenting at once the affaire her Gallic soul
adored, and feeling happy because not even her money could
bring Mademoiselle the lovers that were the due of every
self-respecting female. Francette had no moral scruples, so
that the fact of Monsieur Trent having a wife troubled her
not at all. Were not men just men all the world over, and
since the Good God, by means of the Great War, had seen
fit to remove a good fifty per cent. of the world's prospective
husbands, mustn't women make the best of what they could
get, and make hay while the sun shone?
Nicky had seen the smile and understood it well enough.
It made him vaguely uncomfortable and ill-at-ease-admit-
ted him, as it were, to partnership in a liaison. When he'd
no intention of having anything to do with it, still less

sorry for Carolyn, and because the flattery with which she

Bridal March
surrounded him did him good. She made him feel, as he

Bridal March
139
"And I'd hate to have Celia different in one single detail,

Bridal March
second because in these days a kiss meant nothing anyway.

141
He was furious with himself now, that he'd allowed

Rodney Clifton's parties usually ended in a general

142
Bridal March
informal gatherings, certainly, but gatherings where no one

In Carolyn's house, on the other hand, there was never

There might be ten people, or there might be twenty drop-

Celia scarcely ever threw a party at home. The pent-

and even that meant a crush, with the result that her parties
usually resolved into restaurant dinners or suppers, that

Bridal March
143
of course, but after all-damn it!-a man was only human

She'd write to him, of course. The hysterical sort of letter

didn't mind that. He could cope with letters all right.

that rendered him weak and helpless and uncomfortable,

and certainly never wanted to.
He was glad, genuinely glad, they were going away before
she had time to stage a repetition of a scene he wanted
terribly to forget and couldn't.
Chapter XVI
LIFE in Havana was a very different proposition from
that of New York. It was gay certainly-there is no city
of its size and situation that can be gayer than Havana
during the winter season, but its gayety took a different
144
Bridal March
form, and the young Trents were living under totally dif-
ferent conditions. They had no friends for one thing, and,
as strangers in a strange land, were compelled, for amuse-
ment, to fall back on entertainment that catered for the
general public, on the Casino, or hotel dances, on the racing
at Oriental Park and on the bathing, rather than on the pri-
vate parties to which they'd been accustomed during the
last three hectic months in New York.
To Celia the change came as a definite relief. It was no
part of Charles Betteridge's scheme to bring with him an
assistant who wasn't prepared to pull her weight, and in
choosing Celia he'd known he could rely on her loyalty
to her job as well as on her eager coöperation in the object
he had nearest to what passed for his heart, which was to
make of the new branch a success that would at least justify
its existence. It was at his suggestion that they'd given up
the idea of the flat which had been part of their original
plan, and remained on in the hotel, where they'd found
what they had intended to be temporary accommodation.
"He's right too, I expect," Celia said, talking it over with
Nicky a couple of days after their arrival. "This job's going
to be bigger than I thought, and if you don't mind, Nicky-
it is going to be easier really. Not quite so nice, perhaps, but
it won't be for long. Once things get going and straight"
"I wonder," Nicky said slowly. He was like most other
Englishmen of his type in his dislike of hotel life, in his
affection for things about him that were his own, and for
the friendly intimacies of a home that were impossible in
a hotel. "Sometimes I'm inclined to think that things going
straight or not won't make any real difference to us. I'm not
grumbling, mind you, but that's how I feel. We're getting
into a rut, and the chances are that we'll stay in it. After all,
Bridal March
145
as ruts go we're pretty comfortable. You're happy in it at
all events”
"And you?" Celia said. She wished Nicky wouldn't talk
this way, wouldn't make her feel as though she were cheat-

was only the life they'd always intended to live. They'd
thrashed it all out quite thoroughly right at the beginning,
and neither of them, then, had wanted to be tied. Nicky
had been just as sane on that point as she herself then. He'd
been glad enough to have her working, to know that her

because without putting it into words he made one feel quite
