Chapter XVII
CELIA, shown the letter, laughed.
"Carolyn's the most transparent little fool alive, but
one can't help admiring the nerve of a woman who knows
exactly what she wants, and has the courage to go splash-
ing after it as she does. Almost, I think, you ought to feel
a little flattered, darling. Carolyn may be the world's prize
idiot, but one can't get away from the fact that she's an
incredibly wealthy young woman."
"You don't take Carolyn very seriously, do you?"
Nicky said. He'd had a bad night, had been awake for
hours, and he'd got up feeling edgy and irritable. At cross-
purposes with himself, with Celia, because she looked so
fresh and clear-eyed, with the world in general, and with
Carolyn in particular for reminding him of things he hoped
he'd forgotten and for making him look a fool in Celia's
eyes. "I think you're a bit inclined to underestimate her,
you know, Celia."
The moment the words were out he wished he hadn't
said them. What the hell did Carolyn matter anyway, and
what was the good of putting ideas into Celia's head? It
wasn't as though there'd been anything to it either. He'd
taken damned good care of that, so why-in the name of all
idiocy-had he made a remark like that? But Celia was
feeling happy this morning, impervious to hints flung out
by an edgy, bad-tempered husband for the express purpose
Bridal March
153
of annoying her, and the remark only amused her. Sending
her eyebrows up into little arches over her laughing, teasing
eyes.
"Ought I to take her seriously? Oh, Nicky, I'm sorry.
I didn't mean to spoil the game. Honestly I didn't. Is
Carolyn really one of my hated rivals?"
Nicky said slowly:
"You're very sure of yourself, aren't you, my dear? And
of me? Which is a mistake always." He was conscious of

him on to say things carefully calculated to hurt badly.
"No woman with any sense can ever afford to take chances

Chapter XVIII
ACTUALLY the play was finished at the end of December,
and in January, three days after it came back from the
typists, Raymond Stelland wrote that he was in New York
preparatory to the production of a new show and to hunt
up a couple of good new plays, and that he planned to take
a run down to Havana and see what the place was like.
"I'm still waiting," he wrote, "to hear what you've
done with your own play, which I thought you were going
to let me read. Perhaps, if you've a copy handy, I could
run through it while I'm down there."
Nicky said:
"If Stelland takes it, I'm as good as made. His name's
a byword. Nothing he does is ever known to flop. Just
think of it, Celia. If I could just get Stelland interested"
But Raymond Stelland, when he came, proved a bitter
disappointment. He took the play with him to his own
hotel, and next morning, quite early, he came round to
the Atlanta and brought it back. It was Sunday, and,
because for once the office didn't need her, Celia was in
the patio with Nicky when he arrived. It was perfectly
obvious even before he spoke that he wasn't going to take
the play.
He sat down opposite them at the little green table and
banged it with his fist, so that the glasses it held jingled
and rattled.
"But what the devil's got you?" he said. "When I read
the first act I thought you'd got hold of a decent thing.
I thought the play had even the makings of a brilliant show,
and now-Good Lord, it's sentimental trash. What in the
name of all the Saints have you done with it?"
Bridal March
167
"Made it human," Nicky said quietly. "I've been afraid
of this. Ever since I finished it, but it's got to stand just
the same. I believe-although I don't expect you to agree
with me-that there's a market for stuff like mine. Even
in these days. That there's still room in the world for
decency and cleanliness and honesty, and that underneath
all the pose of sophistication there are still people-and
plenty of them-who prefer a decent healthy love story
to sex stuff most modern dramatists think they want.
Look at Bitter Almonds for example"
"Bitter Almonds," Raymond Stelland said impatiently,
"is a hit because Dorothy Warrington plays the lead, and
where Dorothy Warrington goes there'll always be a section
of the public who'll follow her. The story's as old as the
hills"
"All love stories are as old as the hills," Nicky said.
"And age doesn't alter them any more than it detracts
from the beauty of the hills. It's only a question of han-
dling, and I believe I've treated my story from a new
angle"
Raymond Stelland said, interrupting a second time:
"Every Tom, Dick or Harry who writes a play thinks
he's treated an old story from a new angle. That's one of
the diseases every dramatist's subject to. It's like measles,
and just as prevalent. And the public isn't interested in
old stories. Whether they're treated from a new angle or
not-"

"How many producers have tested it to see whether it's

and Raymond Stelland shook his head.

Bridal March
It's come to rely on me for something out of the

have pineapple juice, won't you, Celia? What's yours,
Stelland?"
For three months after that the play traveled backwards
and forwards to and from the States, and in nearly every
instance managers sent it back with the same criticism
accompanying it. Mr. Clifton or Mr. Harris or Mr. Thomas,
as the case might be, had read the play, but regretted that
while the dialogue was excellent and the plot displayed
quite an admirable knowledge of the theater, the story
itself was too old, too hackneyed and too sentimental for
their use. They would, however, be glad to be given an
opportunity to read any further work of Mr. Trent's.
At its tenth return, no longer the trim, neatly-bound
copy that had started out on its travels, but a dog's-eared,

thumb-marked effigy of itself, Nicky re-addressed it once

Bridal March
171
a baby or two, which would be exciting for the time being,

And then at the end of March came a second letter from

"Last night," she wrote, "I heard a man talking about

Chapter XIX
WATCHING the play in rehearsal was a devastating proceed-
ing. Sitting there in the sheeted stalls of a gloomy, empty
heater, Nicky thought he'd never heard quite such appalling
tripe uttered on any stage by any company as he listened
to during those heart-breaking weeks of preparation for the
opening night of Chiefly Mary, nor seen any company quite
so bad. Twice already he'd made changes in the cast, and
Peter Malcolm, who'd taken on the play's direction, was
ready to tear his hair in speechless despair. It wasn't, either,
that they'd had to try to cut expenses to a minimum, or to
employ a cheap cast. Carolyn had been more than generous
in the matter of money. On the day following Nicky's ar-
rival in New York, and the interview when they'd decided
to go through with the play, she'd come to his hotel quite
early in the morning with a pass-book and a check-book.
"I've opened an account in your name for fifty thousand
dollars, and I want you to do with it just as you like,
Nicky. I don't want you to stint yourself or the production
in anything, and if that's not enough there's heaps more.
Oh, Nicky, I'm so terribly grateful to you for letting me
do this."
Nicky said, a little embarrassed and uncomfortable in her
company, because he always felt that in fairness to Celia
and to Carolyn herself, he must keep careful watch on
everything he said or did:
"It's I who ought to be grateful, Carolyn. There isn't
a living soul who'd have done a thing like this for me,"
and Carolyn had answered quite simply and straightly:
"Perhaps there's no one who loves you quite as I do," and
then had caught herself up rather sharply with a little wist-
Bridal March
175
ful look in her eyes that made him feel a brute and a beast.

Bridal March
me, and once I get up there again there's bound to be

And so, with fifty thousand dollars in the bank in his

Chapter XX
It was Francette, coming in to wake him with his early

"Darling, I know you do. Only at our age we ought

Lying awake in the darkness, with her voice still filling

Chapter XXI
FROM where he stood in his corner of the foyer Nicky
watched the people arriving. They came in taxis, in private
cars, on foot. Women in ermine and diamonds, in dyed
rabbit and synthetic pearls, in "afternoon frocks" and, for
the upper circle and gallery, in tweeds and little squash
hats that could easily be taken off and rolled into a bundle
and stowed away under seats. Escorted here and there by
men in tails, or dinner-jackets; for the most part, since the
American male regards dressing at night, except for a
formal dinner or ball, as one of the foibles attributable
to Englishmen, by men in lounge suits and colored shirts
and ties. All of them with at least one point in common,
which was to be amused or at all events interested. The
majority of them were inveterate first-nighters, men and
women who made a point of being present at the opening
of any play, be it good, bad or indifferent. They formed
a little group of their own, wearing no insignia of office,
but invariably distinguishable. Making of the theater a duty
rather than a pleasure, greeting the manager by name,
being greeted in return. Either seasoned theatergoers who,
having seen all there is to see, continue to practice their
Bridal March
203
profession in the forlorn hope that somewhere they will
find something new, or those who regard a first night
merely as an occasion for a social gathering, and after the
play is over can, in nine cases out of ten, barely remember
what they've seen. Here and there amongst them were the
critics. They would be sitting in aisle seats, so that when
they'd seen enough on which to base their several criticisms,
or had endured enough to justify them in damning the
play as a failure, they could slip out without stepping on
people's toes and make their bored way back to the office
of the paper, and so to bed. And in the intervals they'd
congregate in little groups in the foyer and strip from the
play any rags of decency which it might once have boasted,
and leave it behind them a jibbering, rotting bundle of
bones that when one looked at it made one feel a little
nauseated and repelled.
Nicky knew one or two of them by sight. Jeffrey Mirfield,
whose bitter caustic tongue flayed alike players, plays and
playwrights-who, standing firm and secure on the pedestal
of his own omnipotence, of his own inviolability, feared
neither God nor man. Least of all, authors and actors.
David Rotherburn, who was kinder in his phrasing, less
bitter perhaps, but no less hard to please. George Aylesbury,
who liked musical shows without too much dressing of the
chorus, and was, so his colleagues asserted, the best judge
of legs and bodies in the entire United States. They came
later than the rest of the crowd, finding in the theater their
business rather than their enjoyment, seeing no sense in
clocking-in before time, passing Nicky in his corner with-
out so much as a passing glance, commenting idly, indif-
ferently, on the cast, on the author, and on the play itself
as they went by.

"They say Cecily Hockley walked out on it. What d'you

Backstage the atmosphere had taken on the ominously
dangerous silence that, in a tropical country, is invariably
the forerunner of storms and hurricanes and volcanic up-
heavals, and is typical of any theater on a first night. In
her dressing-room Kathleen Dunsford, in the grip of in-
cipient hysteria, was staging the only display of tempera-
ment she'd shown from start to finish. Not even the
knowledge that on her dressing-room door was painted the
little red star that is the aim and ambition of every young
actor and actress, not the all-pervading scent of the magnifi-
cent basket of roses and lilies and carnations which Nicky
had sent her, and which was the first she'd ever had sent

fact that she could remember not even the first word of
her own first entrance. Across the passage Walter Heron
was calm, with the calmness of utter resignation to the
inevitable. He was, as he knew quite well, and as the critics
had said at regular intervals for the best part of seven
years, a really first-rate actor, but even first-rate actors are
to be found in flops, and if the play flopped-as it seemed
Bridal March
205
fairly certain it must-it wouldn't be the first time a good
actor had played lead in a failure, and probably wouldn't
be the last either. He was sorry for Nicky Trent, because
really the play was quite charming. With Cecily Hockley
heading the cast, there'd been every reason to suppose that
it would at least have survived a run of-well of a couple

all the theaters excepting those housing the absolute top-

Worked like a little navvy she had, right from the start,
but she'd no name, no draw, and the play needed the
fillip of a drawing name. It was too simple, too clean and
straightforward to stand on its own feet these days. The
public didn't want simplicity unless it was backed by a
popular star, and it had no use for nice, clean, love stories
with their only appeal that of sentiment and youth and a
charming story. He could see already the sort of things
Jeffrey Mirfield would say, and they weren't reassuring,
wondered why he had stayed in the show himself, and
was rather surprised, on careful analysis of his feelings, to
realize that he'd stayed because he'd liked the play, and
because he'd enjoyed the part allotted to him. Queer, he
thought, putting the last touches of make-up to eyes and
mouth, the reasons one had for what one did.

Two hours earlier at the flying field, Nicky and Celia had
met for the first time for six weeks. The 'plane bringing
her had been twenty minutes late, and he'd known sudden
hideous panic. Suppose anything happened-she'd insisted
on flying up because even for so important an occasion
as the first night of Nicky's play there was no time to
be lost in boats and trains-suppose there was an accident!
One heard of horrible things, of 'planes falling, inexplicably,
into the sea, of forced landings and hideous subsequent
fires. If anything happened to Celia-
He'd lived again, minute by minute, through the whole
six weeks of their separation during those twenty minutes
of agonized waiting. Through their folly, their hopes and
fears, their disappointments, switching onwards a little to
the certainty of failure that waited for him inside the next

Bridal March
213
Can you tell me that? A lot of sentimental boloney, that's

And that in effect was what, next morning, the majority
of the critics called it too. They were universal in their
praise of the dialogue, and of Kathleen Dunsford, who had
"established herself overnight as an actress of the highest
rank"; they spoke warmly, as critics always spoke, of Walter
Heron's part in a production: "Mr. Heron as usual dis-
played his customary perfection of representation," and all
of them worthy of the name of critic said the same thing
of the play. Varying the wording of their reports, certainly,
but meaning the same.
"The author of Chiefly Mary has been wise to choose
the spring for his presentation of a play so simple in plot,
in construction and in story as to be almost an insult to
the intelligence of the average theater-goer, since in the
spring it is commonly asserted and an accepted fact that
the minds of men and maidens-and in many cases of
those old enough to know better-lightly turn to thoughts
of love. The story to which a packed and absorbed theater
listened last night is thin to the point of emaciation, but
the fact remains that underlying its almost childlike sim-
plicity and honesty there is that unnameable, indefinable
quality which for want of a more explicit word we call
charm, and there is in it too that which makes hardened
reprobates resolve in future to abandon their sinfulness,
and brings facile tears to the eyes of even the most cynical
critic. Despite its simplicity, its lack of everything that goes
to the making of what the public has learnt to expect from
a popular play, there is little doubt but that Mr. Trent's
first effort is in for a long and highly-successful run."

"I-see."

are tired their brains don't work normally. They see things

When he woke next morning, the room was full of
sunlight. It streamed in through wide-open, uncurtained
windows, making him blink sharply, and he rolled over in
bed, rubbing his eyes. Lord, but he'd slept well, which
was the more surprising, because with all the excitement

on his mind, Celia-and Carolyn.
He wondered if Celia were already awake, whether,
despite all the upset, she'd slept as soundly as he had.
She'd been right, of course, to leave over till the morning
the discussion which was inevitable between them. At night

Bridal March
221
in the daylight, one's viewpoint became dulled and obscured

He knew now what he'd do. Last night was different.

He turned over so that his eyes might feast for a moment

follow me there. Our only chance for the future lies in my
222
Bridal March
going away like this for a bit, so that I can think detachedly
and decide what to do. If I stay here and see you I shall
remember always the things that-if we are ever to be
happy together again-it is imperative I should forget.
Please believe, Nicky, that this is really the only way."
Chapter XXII
LOOKING back on it all afterwards, Celia knew that the
decision to leave him had been there all along. That her
appeal for a night in which to think things over had only
been in the nature of a compromise. Stalling, as it were,
because right from the beginning, from that hideous minute
when Nicky, standing there by the door, had brought
Carolyn into the conversation, she'd known that only one
path lay ahead of her.
Nicky, miraculously, had gone to sleep. Not at once,
certainly, but before very long. As always Nicky had
the gift of going to sleep despite any crisis or upheaval.
Over and over again she'd envied him that ability to put
sleep between himself and any worries, and even now,
faced with stark, staring tragedy, the gift hadn't deserted
him. He'd lain awake, tossing a little from side to side,
for perhaps an hour, and then he'd gone to sleep as usual,
leaving Celia awake to wrestle with the problem that was
to settle the future of both their lives. And instantly she'd
known that the only way to settle it was to go away there
and then. To leave him now, at once, while he was asleep,
so that neither the sight of his hurt eyes nor the sound
of his voice imploring her, could have the power to in-
fluence a decision she knew to be the only possible solution
to the whole hideous business.
Bridal March
223
With the decision made it had all been extraordinarily

"I forgot to tell you to have me called at three," she

a taxi?"
Inside the taxi she'd given the only address she could
think of on the spur of the moment.
"Pennsylvania station, driver. The departure side,"
she'd said. One could wait at a station without looking
conspicuous. There, at a big terminus like that, no one

flying field, but in the meantime-
At the station she found a buffet open, went in and

224
Bridal March
still be going on as though nothing had happened. The

They were tired of each other, and bored, and so the
machinery of the divorce court with all its unpleasant
method of churning up mud and grime, had to be brought
in to cut in two a knot that, tied so lightly, so happily,
so hopefully, could only be severed in disgrace and scandal
and dishonor.
The village was disappointed in the young Trents. Glad
that they were apparently going to stay away for the
future. Feeling as it did on the subject, it would have
been awkward, terribly awkward, to have had either of
Bridal March
235
them back, living there at the Little House, where, day in,
day out, there must be at least a certain amount of social
intercourse. The Rector's wife expressed the opinion quite
frankly that the village in general had deep cause for
thankfulness in that it had been spared the painful neces-
sity of showing either of the delinquents how definitely
it disapproved of the modern method of divorce.
And now, out of a clear blue sky, unheralded, utterly
unexpected, Nicky had come home. Late last night he
had turned up at the Marton Arms, asked for a room,
had dinner all alone, gone out for a walk, and then gone
straight to bed. Dick Rogers, who was the landlord, had
wanted terribly to talk to him. In the old days, young Mr.
Trent had often dropped in for a drink at the bar, and
had been a pleasant, nice-spoken, cheery young gentleman
then, but now-Dick Rogers had thought better of trying
to get him into conversation last night, of making any
remarks other than those definitely called for in the course
of business, of asking even one of the questions he was so
longing to ask. Because obviously something had hap-
pened to young Mr. Trent. He looked, Dick said afterwards
in the bar, twenty years older than when he went away, and
-kind of grim. All his jolly ways gone and nothing left but
black silence, and the sort of expression that said as clearly
as words could have said it: "I'll thank you, all of you,
to leave me alone, and not to say a word to me more than's
absolutely necessary," and they'd left him alone, though
the news of his arrival had spread like wildfire through
the village. Quite alone, as, by his manner, it was more
than evident he wanted to be left.
But it is doubtful if, just then, Nicky knew what he
really wanted. Whether he could have told any one what he
honestly wanted from life save that Celia might come
236
Bridal March
back to him, which was, of course, a wish so crazy that,
instinctively, one strangled it at birth. It was odd how
persistently Celia lived in him, even now, when the divorce

or other bring her back, reminding him of things sanity

and slipped out by a side entrance, because when she still
didn't come and he asked a cleaner for her, the woman

days, too. The scheme that she and that queer old chap,
Betteridge, had concocted together had, rather surprisingly,
gone through with flying colors. They'd had the idea of
buying a huge house that stood in its own park, of turning
it into a terribly exclusive hotel, of engaging first-rate
orchestras, golf and tennis professionals, of making it
almost a country club, and the Company had seen in Celia
the right person to be the new hotel's first manageress.
For the two winters that had followed its inception, the
place had been packed. America, old as well as young, had
been willing to pay anything for rooms, and the Company
had signalized its approval by putting Celia on its Board
of Directors. She was the first woman ever to sit on the
Bridal March
237
Board, and her appointment had been commented upon
rather conspicuously by the Press. She had written, at the
request of a popular woman's journal, a series of articles
on "Women in Business," and they had been published
with her photograph inset. Taken in one of her dark
English tailor-mades that looked so simple and were yet
so expensive, a still lovely young woman who looked
out at the world through eyes that were astonishingly
clear and full of level-headed, business-like common sense.
Nicky pictured her sometimes, could see her sitting in
the Board Room at Directors' meetings, running her hotel
with her usual quiet, methodical determination, would
have given his soul for the right to take her into his arms,
to kiss from the eyes that looked out from her photo-

cessful business-woman, and to bring back to them the
lovelight with which he had known so well how to fill
them, and knew that by an hour of crazy folly he had for
ever forfeited even the right to her friendship, let alone
her love.
Leaving old Merriman's office, his feet turned instinctively
in the direction of the Little House. He told himself

instead of funking it like this," and tramped on. It was
early autumn, but already the leaves were falling, and
last night it had rained, so that all over the road lay little
heaps of sodden brown leaves that added, if there was
room for any addition, to the general depression already
deepening in his mind. The village brought everything
back so vividly. He and Celia picnicking in the empty

238
Bridal March
despite all the silly arguments they had about all sorts of

From Celia his thoughts went, automatically, to Carolyn.
Poor, little, loving Carolyn, who'd asked so little of life,

"Let me see her, Nicky. I'll make her understand. Oh,

But Celia had refused point-blank to see her, to have

"I have nothing to say either to you or Nicky beyond

Bridal March
In the end they were married before a Justice of the
Peace in Connecticut. With Francette and Carolyn's
chauffeur as witnesses, and with a thunderstorm blazing
and crackling and roaring overhead. And in November
Nicky's son was stillborn and Carolyn died. Quietly and
unostentatiously as she had lived, slipping out of the world
so silently, so unexpectedly, that even the nurse sitting by
the fire at the other end of the room had not seen the
change until too late, had not known she was going until
she had actually gone.
For two years and a half after that day, Nicky had stayed
on in New York. Not because he particularly wanted to
stay there, but because, since he cared so little where he
lived, one place seemed as good as another. Nothing had
mattered much, it seemed in those days, and the desire
for Celia grew worse with every day that passed. He went,
once, after Carolyn's death, to the hotel in Havana, had
seen her again and again, had talked with her, but there
it had ended. She had been charming, but absolutely
definite in her decision to let sleeping dogs lie. She'd said:
"Why don't you be sensible and accept the inevitable,
Nicky? I can't take you back. Not as I feel about things;
and what's the use of all this? I don't even want you back
now at least not in my sane moments"
"But there are moments-aren't there when you're not
sane?" Nicky said eagerly. He grasped as a drowning man
grasps at a straw that passes him, at anything that might
bring her a shade nearer. "You'll admit that, won't you?"
and she nodded.
"Why bother to deny it? Most women are hoarders by
nature, and I'm no exception. The most practical of us
keeps an emotional glory-hole somewhere in her makeup,
and when she feels sentimental goes back to it. There
Bridal March
241
are days when I despise mine, when I definitely decide to
have a thorough turn-out and get rid of the rubbish I've
got stored up there, and then-again-well, I don't, that's
all. But the very fact that I've got a glory-hole ought to
prove to you how impossible it is to expect me to come
back to you. They're not only treasures that get pushed
into its corners, you know, Nicky."
He'd not gone back there again. It seemed so silly,
somehow, to hurt oneself unnecessarily, to go out of the
way in search of unhappiness, when it found its way so
easily to one's door. And so for two and a half years
after Carolyn's death he'd stayed on, killing time in New
York, and then, because it seemed equally silly to keep
on a house to which it was quite obvious that neither he
nor Celia would ever return, he wrote to her for instructions
with regard to her own things still left there, and came over
to settle the matter himself.
Turning the corner into the lane that led to the Little
House, the pain of the memories it brought back was almost
intolerable. Every yard of that lane had helped to make
its own particular bit of history, had known scraps of the
life he and Celia had shared there. There was a stone on
one side, a queer mushroom-shaped thing, which Celia
declared belonged to the Little People. One crazy night,
when there'd been a full moon, she'd insisted on his coming
out with her and sitting there for hours in the hope they'd
catch some of them at their midnight frolic, and instead
all they'd caught had been incredibly bad colds. Further
on was the edge of the old disused gravel-pit, which they'd
turned into a grave for their first disreputable old car.
Celia had said:
"It's been a good old bus, and it's got to have a decent
end. Not just left to rot like lots of old cars are left. I'd
242
Bridal March
like to smash it up-have one great and glorious crash,
so that it could die in harness, so to speak. How can we
arrange for a great and glorious crash without involving
ourselves, d'you suppose, Nicky?"
Ultimately they had decided on the gravel pit, and on
the night the new car was delivered they had driven the
old one out of the shed that in those days did duty for a
garage, and taken it out to the edge of the pit. There
they had got out, and standing beside it Nicky had shoved
it into top gear, released the brakes and let it go. Sent
it crashing, rattling, banging its way over the edge, down
the side and into a heap of indistinguishable scrap-iron at
the bottom, while Celia, with becoming gravity, sang the
"Dead March."
The memories were all so ridiculous, so childish, and yet
so happy. The whole place was full of them. He hoped,
coming in sight of the Little House itself, that the old girl
he and Celia had left in charge of the place wouldn't want
to talk and ask questions, that he'd meet no one he knew,
that he'd be able to get through all he had to do during
the day, and clear out again. Another night in the place
would just about finish him. Even last night had been
bad enough, because Dick Rogers had done his best to
pump him, and he wasn't going to be pumped. Not if
he knew it. What had happened lay between him and
Celia and concerned no one else. Not even Carolyn any
longer, poor little thing.
His thoughts switched for a second from Celia to
Carolyn. He wished vaguely she hadn't had to die quite
like that. It seemed so unfair, that, lonely all her life, she
should even die lonely. With no one even to hold her hand
or steady her arm as she stepped down into the icy-cold
water of the river she had to cross. He'd done his best
Bridal March
243
to make her happy during those last months. That, at

Abreast, at last, of the familiar white gate that led into

bad, soaked and saturated as it was in memories he was

Here, through the same little gate, he and Celia had come

had come back to the cozy intimacy of home at the end of

and planned. With five shillings' worth of bulbs from Wool-

Smithson to carry out orders, with all the money they
wanted, to buy special seeds and bulbs, to make of the
wilderness they had taken over the garden of their dreams.
The bird-bath Celia had made out of a hollow stone she'd

A couple of sparrows were flipping about in it now, flirting

Bridal March

He sat on alone after she'd gone, with his back to the
window in his old familiar place. Outside in the garden
a blackbird broke suddenly into a torrent of song; through
the door that led out to the little patch of crazy paving
he and Celia had so grandiosely christened the terrace,
came the scent of flowers, mingled with the smell of the
coffee Mrs. Chively was making in the kitchen. He sat
staring down the length of the table. To where, at the
bottom, Celia had been wont to sit, facing him, so that
always he might revel in her beauty and her charm and
her desirability. Celia who had gone, whom his own reck-
Bridal March
251
less, insensate folly had forced out of his life. He dropped
his head into his hands, unable to face that empty chair
where Celia used to sit, and gave himself up utterly to the
despair that had been closing in on him steadily ever since
he had made himself come back to the Little House where,
together, they'd been so happy. And because he couldn't
trust himself to speak or let Mrs. Chively see his eyes,
pushed away his chair when he heard her coming in with
the coffee, and went over to stand beside the window,
staring out into the garden, with his hands clenched deep
inside his pockets in a desperate endeavor to keep on
himself the hold he was fast losing altogether.
Mrs. Chively behind him, said, solicitous for his comfort:
"I'd drink your coffee while it's hot if I was you, sir,"
and he spoke to her over his shoulder. Scarcely daring
to trust his voice even so short a distance.
"Put it on the table, will you, Mrs. Chively." Anything
to get her out of the room, to be by himself, to fight the
horror alone so that no one should know, should see the
agony he suffered. "I'll help myself when I'm ready."
And then, because he knew he'd reached the uttermost
ends of his tether, went through the open window into the
garden. And sat there hunched into a heap on the little
wooden bench at the foot of the rose garden, alone with
his thoughts, with the wreckage that was left of a life
that ought to have been so splendid.
Afterwards he never remembered how long he'd been
sitting there till he looked up and saw Celia staring at
him from just inside the garden gate. That it was dark
he knew, because Mrs. Chively had switched on the lamp
outside the front door, and the hard white light fell full
on her where she stood. With her hand still on the latch
of the gate, and her eyes full of something to which he
252
Bridal March
didn't even dare to give a name. She said, speaking slowly:
"I'm sorry about this, Nicky. Only Lennie told me you
weren't even in England. I was having tea with her, and
I told her I was coming here. She said quite definitely
that you were still in the States"
"Lennie said that," Nicky said. He got up from the
bench, still staring at her, came over and stood beside
her at the little white gate. "But Lennie" He stopped
short, an idea blazing its way into his misery-laden brain.
Oh, blessed, thrice-blessed interfering Lennie. Who'd made
quite sure where he would be, that there could be no
chance of their missing each other. Her reiterated persistent
question: "You're sure you can't get away by that time,
Nicky?" and his own impatient reply: "My dear girl, I'll
be here till ten or eleven. I tell you I'm busy." She'd
known, of course, that Celia would have refused to meet
him under normal conditions, and so she'd pulled the wires
her own way. He said: "I thought Lennie knew I was at
home again, but probably I didn't tell her. I don't go out
much these days anywhere."
"I know," Celia said. She shut the gate behind her and
stood leaning against it. Her eyes were utterly inscrutable,
her voice level and devoid of every shade of emotion. "I
was sorry about-Carolyn. It must have come as a great
shock-her dying like that. Are you staying here, Nicky?"
"No," Nicky said. "I thought you knew I was coming
home to arrange about the sale. I wrote you"
Celia nodded.
"I know. It was Lennie. She said you'd arranged to
leave it a little longer-that you wouldn't be here till next
month. I took it for granted she knew. Unless I'd felt
quite certain of that I shouldn't have come here tonight
like this. You know that, don't you?"
Bridal March
253
Nicky said slowly:
"You're like me, I expect. Wanted to see the house
again. Queer, isn't it, what a hold this place has got on
us both? Until I came today, I never realized how much it
meant to me. It's so full of things one can't forget"
"Oh-don't," Celia said, and caught her breath sharply
on something that might almost have been a sob. "I mean
-there's no sense in going over it all again, Nicky. I was a
fool to come, really"
Nicky said irrelevantly:
"There's some coffee in the dining-room. It's probably
cold, but I dare say we could drink it. Or find something
else. And since you are here-can't we make the most of
it? The place is in the devil of a mess, because I'm cleaning
out, but we needn't stand out here to say what we have
to say, need we? After all, the house is your home as
well as mine."
"There's nothing to say, really," Celia said, but she fol-
lowed him none the less through the open window into
the dining-room beyond. "I came-oh, because I suppose
I'm a sentimental idiot. I wanted to see the place before
you sold it, to say good-by to it"
"As we've said good-by to so many things," Nicky said
slowly. He poured out a cup of coffee, brought it over to
her, fetched another cup and poured it out for himself,
drinking it at a gulp. It was stone cold, but he didn't even
know it. "What a mess we've made of things, you and I,
haven't we, Celia? We'd so much each other, all the
money we wanted, this house-and you wanted a career,
and I wanted" He shrugged his shoulders, and Celia
filled the gap.
"Carolyn," she said briefly, but he contradicted her.
"No. You're wrong there. Utterly wrong. I tried to

Bridal March
make you see that at the time, only you wouldn't listen.
That night-we know each other well enough to call a

I didn't want Carolyn. Not particularly. First and fore-

That and the noise together. And you turned me down.
You were too busy. You thought I was behaving like an
hysterical overwrought child. Well, perhaps I was. I dare

with an overwrought child, and you couldn't, that night,
reason with me. I went to Carolyn, because she was the
one person I could rely on there and then to provide
the peace of mind and body I had to have or go mad.
Carolyn is dead. She died, primarily, because she was
too generous to deny me anything it was in her power to

afterwards. When it was too late for the knowledge to
be of any good. She knew that if she ever had a child
she would, in all probability, die. There was something
terribly wrong, internally wrong, only she didn't care. She
was one of those women born to be mothers, and she
was willing to take the risk. Well, she took it, for my
sake, and she died. And because she's dead I can't say
what I'd like to say. That much honor, at least, I can

it herself. Always. Even that night. I never loved her.
Either then or afterwards. I married her because she was

sounds thin-but I have never loved any woman in my
life except you, Celia. You were the first woman, as you
definitely will be the last. To all intents and purposes,
Carolyn doesn't matter. When other men find compensa-
Bridal March
255
tion for things-anywhere-in a casual affair with the

He came over to stand beside her, so that she could see
the twisted lines of his unhappy mouth and the tragedy
in his eyes. "The last time I said this to you I swore I
would never say it again. I told myself that a man must
keep at least the rags of his pride, but since then-I've
begun to wonder whether even the rags matter, and I
know they don't. I know that out of the whole world
only one thing is worth even thinking twice about, and
that's you, and what we could have made of our lives if
I hadn't been a fool, and you hadn't thought a career more
important than love. I swore then that I would never ask
you again to come back to me, and now I know that I
can't keep the vow I made. We're still young. Both of us.
We've the best part of our lives still ahead of us, and
Carolyn's dead. But you and I are left. We're here. In the
Little House where we once made such plans and dreamed
such dreams. Once again I beg of you to think before you
give me your final answer. Will you let the dead past bury
its dead, and come back to me, Celia?"
Later, much, much later, with Mrs. Chively in the kitchen
wondering in rather shocked silence what was going to
happen next, and whether she could be haled before the
Courts for not advising the King's Proctor of this surprising
turn of events, Nicky came out from the dining-room
256
Bridal March
into the hall and called to her. His eyes had a light in
them she'd never seen before, and as she told Julia after-
wards, he might have lost ten years off his age.
"I think it's only fair to tell you, Mrs. Chively," he said,
"because it's quite possible you don't know the difference
between English and American divorce laws, and you may
think all of this-well, a bit odd. You see, Mrs. Trent and
I were only divorced under the American law, so that our
English marriage stands. And I wanted to tell you. First
of all, because I'm sure you'll be glad to hear the news.
The Little House isn't going to be sold after all. We're
going to live here. Mrs. Trent and I. For the rest of our
lives I hope. You see, she's coming back to me."
Behind him Celia spoke over his shoulder. "There's a
message I've got to send. Now," she said. "A cable to the
office." She went across the pretty square hall, treading as
always Nicky had pictured her treading. As though her
feet scarcely touched the ground. Picked up the telephone
that stood on the table in the corner and dictated, clearly
and decisively, the message she wished to send.
"Marton 98 speaking," she said, and across the hall her
eyes met Nicky's, holding them fast. "A night letter cable-
gram, please, operator. To 'Ships. New York City.' Yes.
S-H-I-P-S. It's a cable address. Got it? Yes. All right.
Here's the message. 'Have to advise that urgent private
affairs necessitate my remaining permanently in England.
Letter follows. Durham.' Yes, that's right. Durham. No.
D for duty. Got it? Read it back to me, will you? Thanks.
That's right. Good night, operator."
She put back the receiver on its rest, walked across the
hall and straight into the arms Nicky held out to take her.
"Oh, Nicky. Don't you understand even now? I'm not
coming back, my darling. I've come."
